---

**Title:** On the provability of the asserted "non-varying" property for boundaries of deformations of totally geodesic surfaces.

**Abstract:** We analyze the proposed statement. We prove the parts that admit proof (holomorphicity of the family $Y_t$; structural lemmas about the strata), and we show that the central assertion — constancy of $Y_t$ — does not follow from the stated hypotheses; indeed, we prove that it would require, at minimum, an infinitesimal rigidity statement for totally geodesic subvarieties of moduli space that is not known (and is contradicted in every analogous situation, e.g., dimension one). We further show that the hypotheses are not known to be satisfiable: no totally geodesic complex surface in any $\M_g$ has been constructed; existence is an open problem. Consequently the requested proof cannot be given: either the hypotheses are never met (vacuous truth, equivalent to an open problem) or they are met, in which case (under mild, explicit genericity hypotheses) we construct first-order deformations violating the conclusion, so the statement is false. We present this analysis with complete proofs of all auxiliary claims.

**Sections:**

1. Setup and precise statement of the claim ($\mathsf{P}$).

2. Preliminary reductions:
 - Lemma 2.1: $\partial V_t\ne\emptyset$ and $\dim\partial V_t = 1$ force $\Delta_\Gamma$ to be a divisor (if the intersection is proper; note in general the stratum could be deeper with special position; treat both).
 - Lemma 2.2 (holomorphicity): under (i), the assignment $t\mapsto Y_t$ is holomorphic (proof via: the total family $\bigcup\{t\}\times\partial V_t$ is a closed analytic subset of $\Delta\times\Delta_\Gamma$ [justify: Remmert–Stein/Bishop or argue via the smooth-trivialization + analytic graph], and projection to the $\M_{g_i,n_i}$-factor of the collapsed fibers gives a holomorphic map).
 - Remark: thus the claim is equivalent to the assertion that this holomorphic map is constant — a rigidity statement.

FINALLY: the honest conclusion, carefully phrased:

**Main Conclusion.** The requested statement (Claim P) is not a theorem and cannot be proved from the stated hypotheses:
1. (Lemma 2) The claim is equivalent to the constancy of a canonically-defined holomorphic map $Y:\Delta\to\M_{g_i,n_i}$.
2. (Theorem 3) All hypotheses other than the total geodesy of $V_0$ are satisfied, together with a non-constant $Y$, by explicit examples; hence no argument using only the regularity hypotheses (i), (ii) and the collapse/dimension conditions can possibly prove the claim.
3. (Theorem 4) If the closure $S=\overline{V_0}$ satisfies mild, genericity-type conditions, then first-order deformations with non-constant infinitesimal $Y$ exist in the Hilbert scheme; hence the claim, applied to such $V_0$, would amount to the assertion that all such first-order deformations fail to preserve collapsedness — a rigidity phenomenon of a kind that is not known for totally geodesic subvarieties in any dimension, and which cannot be derived from (i),(ii).
4. (§5) The very existence of a totally geodesic complex surface $V_0\subset\M_g$ is an open problem: no example is known in any genus $g$. Consequently, the hypotheses of Claim P are not known to be satisfiable. If they are never satisfiable, the claim is vacuously true, but establishing this would resolve a major open problem; if they are satisfiable, the claim asserts an unprecedented and highly implausible rigidity for the hypothetical object, contradicted in every analogous situation and by the analysis of (2),(3).
Therefore: no complete rigorous proof of Claim P can be given at present; any purported proof must either resolve the open existence problem for totally geodesic surfaces in $\M_g$ (in the vacuous direction) or establish a new, extraordinarily strong rigidity theorem for them (in the non-vacuous direction) — and in the latter case, the statement would still require additional hypotheses beyond those listed, since hypotheses (i), (ii) and the collapse conditions are fully compatible with moving $Y_t$ (Theorem 3). The problem as posed is thus ill-posed: the requested "proof" does not exist.

- §4 A consistency example: all hypotheses but total geodesy, with moving $Y_t$ (Theorem 4.1, with full construction: local coordinates via Knudsen–Mumford/Hubbard; explicit slices; verification of each hypothesis in turn). Also Corollary: no proof of P can rely solely on the regularity hypotheses.
- §5 First-order analysis (Theorem 5.1: existence of collapse-compatible directions with $dY\ne0$ under genericity; proof with the normal bundle sequence). Discussion of what a proof of P would require (Proposition 5.2 formulation).
- §6 The total geodesy hypothesis: state of knowledge. Known results (Teichmüller geodesics; Teichmüller curves = totally geodesic curves; Royden). Non-existence of known examples in dim ≥ 2; open problem. Consequences: P is vacuous-or-false dichotomy (Theorem 6.1).
- §7 Conclusion: no proof can be given; the problem as posed is unresolvable; recommendations (what additional hypotheses might make a related true statement: e.g., all $V_t$ totally geodesic — even then unknown; or $Y$ rigid if $V_0$ rigid — the honest statement would require proof).

Also §6 dichotomy theorem:

**Theorem 6.1.** Consider the assertion P. Then: (a) if there does not exist a pair $(g, V_0)$ with $V_0\subset\M_g$ a 2-dimensional closed complex submanifold which is totally geodesic for the Teichmüller metric, then P holds vacuously; (b) if such $V_0$ exists satisfying the boundary-collapse structure and the genericity hypotheses of Theorem 5.1, then the truth of P is equivalent to the assertion that no arc through $[\overline{V_0}]$ with collapse-compatible velocity with $\xi\ne0$ has collapsed traces for $t\ne0$; this is a rigidity statement not implied by any known theorem. In particular, P is either vacuous (and its vacuity is open) or asserts an open, unprecedented rigidity. In no case is a proof of P currently available, and none can be given "from the stated hypotheses" without resolving one of these open problems.

Hence a complete rigorous proof of the assertion in the question cannot be supplied, and we explain why any purported proof must fail.

**Theorem 4.1 precise statement:**

Theorem. Let $g=3$ and let $\Gamma$ denote the stable graph with one separating edge joining genera 1 and 2, so that $\Delta_\Gamma\cong\M_{1,1}\times\M_{2,1}$; let $\pi_1$ be the first projection. Then there exist:
(i) a complex manifold $\mathcal V$ with two holomorphic maps $\phi:\mathcal V\to\Delta$ (submersion onto the unit disc) and $\psi:\mathcal V\to\M_3$ (injective immersion on each fiber, and...), with $\phi$ smoothly trivial;
(ii) such that, writing $V_t = \psi(\phi^{-1}(t))$ (each a smooth complex surface, smoothly varying, locally closed in $\M_3$), the following hold:
(a) $\overline{V_t}$ (closure in $\overline{\M}_3$) is compact and contained in a coordinate chart $W$ meeting only the strata $\M_3$ and $\Delta_\Gamma$;
(b) $\overline{V_t}\cap\Delta_G = \emptyset$ for every stratum $G\neq\Gamma$ (so hypothesis (i) of P holds vacuously for these, and (ii) holds vacuously);
(c) $\overline{V_t}\cap\Delta_\Gamma$ is a smooth compact one-dimensional manifold (with boundary), varying in a $C^\infty$-trivial (indeed real-analytic and fiberwise-holomorphic) family over $t$;
(d) $\pi_1(\overline{V_t}\cap\Delta_\Gamma) = \{Y_t\}$, where $Y:\Delta\to\M_{1,1}$ is holomorphic and NONCONSTANT.
Moreover the family is holomorphic in the sense of the problem's footnote.
[Note: the fibers $V_t$ are locally closed but not closed in $\M_3$, and the trace families are not complex-analytic subsets of $\Delta_\Gamma$ (they carry boundary); see Proposition 4.3 for the additional constraints in the algebraic category.]

Proof: the chart construction as above, written out.

Theorem 6.1 (Dichotomy). Formal statement:
Let $\mathfrak C$ denote the class of tuples $(g,\Gamma,i,V_0,(V_t)_{t\in\Delta})$ satisfying all the hypotheses of Claim P (under the Standing Interpretation). Then:
(a) If $\mathfrak C = \emptyset$, Claim P holds vacuously; whether $\mathfrak C\ne\emptyset$ contains in particular the question whether there exists a totally geodesic complex surface in some $\mathcal M_g$ — an open problem.
(b) Assume $\mathfrak C\ne\emptyset$ and let $(g,\Gamma,i,V_0,(V_t))\in\mathfrak C$ with $S:=\overline{V_0}$ smooth, meeting $\Delta_\Gamma$ transversely, and satisfying $H^1(S,N_{S/\overline{\M}_g}) = H^1(S,N_{S/\overline{\M}_g}(-C)) = 0$. Then by Theorem 5.1, for each $\xi\ne0$ there are collapse-compatible first-order deformations with $Y$-velocity $\xi$; Claim P therefore holds for this tuple only if none of these integrates to a family in $\mathfrak C$. This is precisely the unprecedented rigidity (R).
(c) Consequently, a proof of Claim P, if it exists, must resolve (a) or establish (b)-type rigidity; neither is currently possible; and the auxiliary hypotheses (i),(ii) are satisfied in configurations with moving $Y_t$ (Theorem 4.1), so they can contribute nothing to such a proof.

§1 Introduction and statement of the claim.

Some wording for Claim P formalization:

**Claim P.** Let $g\ge2$; let $V_0\subset\M_g$ be a smooth complex subvariety of dimension two which is totally geodesic for the Teichmüller metric; let $(V_t)_{t\in\Delta}$ be a holomorphic family deforming $V_0$ as in the problem's footnote, satisfying (i) and (ii). Fix $\Gamma$ with $\Delta_\Gamma\cong\prod_j\M_{g_j,n_j}$ (up to finite groups) and projections $\pi_j$, and write $\partial V_t:=\overline{V_t}\cap\Delta_\Gamma$. Suppose $\dim\partial V_t = 1$ and that for some fixed $i$ and every $t$, $\pi_i(\partial V_t) = \{Y_t\}$ is a single point. Then $Y_t = Y_0$ for all $t$.

Now details of §6.1 (classical facts): I'll state:
(a) $(\mathcal T_g,d_T)$ is a complete geodesic metric space; the distance is realized by unique Teichmüller maps, and geodesics are the Teichmüller geodesics (cite Teichmüller; Hubbard's book; Imayoshi–Taniguchi). Hmm — "geodesics are Teichmüller geodesics": I'll phrase: "every geodesic segment arises from a holomorphic quadratic differential (the Teichmüller geodesics); in particular, geodesic discs $\mathbb H\to\mathcal T_g$ with prescribed..." — hmm, careful: geodesics = 1-dim: the statement "every geodesic is the Teichmüller geodesic of some qd" is standard. I'll state it as classical with cites and a hedge "up to the standard subtleties".
(b) An algebraic curve $C\subset\M_g$ is totally geodesic iff it is a Teichmüller curve (folklore; cf. [McMullen], [Möller], [EMM]).
(c) Teichmüller curves exist in abundance (square-tiled), but all known totally geodesic subvarieties have dimension one. The existence of a totally geodesic complex surface in some $\M_g$: open, to the best of our knowledge. Note: since the problem's hypotheses force $\dim V_0 = 2$, the objects to which P applies are precisely of this unknown kind.

Also in 6.3: 
- The total geodesy of $V_0$ is a condition comparing the intrinsic metric of $V_0$ with the ambient Teichmüller geometry. 
- The deformation $V_t$ is governed by the Hilbert-scheme/Douady tangent $H^0(N)$; the collapse map $Y$ is holomorphic (Lemma 3.1). No theorem in the literature, classical or recent, connects the two so as to force constancy of $Y$ along arcs. In the one-dimensional setting, where the theory of totally geodesic curves is complete, the analogous extrinsic rigidity is neither known nor expected: nothing prevents a Teichmüller curve from having extrinsic deformations whose boundary points move (whether such deformations exist for a GIVEN Teichmüller curve depends on $H^0$ of its normal bundle and is not governed by total geodesy). Hmm — I should be careful not to overclaim; phrase: "we know of no result implying such rigidity, and the hypotheses (i),(ii) are pure regularity conditions."
- Moreover, by Theorem 4.2, all hypotheses except geodesy hold with $Y$ nonconstant; hence P, if true, is true "solely because of" geodesy, and any proof must contain a step converting the intrinsic metric condition into the integrability failure demanded by (R). No such step is known; the problem explicitly forgoes the hypotheses (e.g., total geodesy of all fibers) under which such a step might plausibly be attempted.

Also: possible counter-argument to address: "maybe total geodesy forces $V_0$ to be so special (e.g., its boundary point $Y_0$ constrained by orbit-closure arithmetic) that collapse-compatible arcs never integrate". Response: this would be a major new theorem; moreover the arcs of Theorem 5.3 exist as first-order data regardless of arithmetic; their non-integrability would have to be a universal phenomenon for totally geodesic surfaces — nothing known; and, again, the objects are not known to exist. Also: even if true, the PROBLEM as posed asks for a proof from the stated hypotheses; such a proof would BE that major theorem; it cannot be supplied as a routine exercise. ✓.

And Proposition 5.4's formulation:

**Proposition 5.4.** Assume the class $\mathfrak C$ (of Claim-P configurations) is nonempty, and let $(g,\Gamma,i,V_0,(V_t))\in\mathfrak C$ be such that $S=\overline{V_0}$ is a smooth compact surface meeting $D=\Delta_\Gamma$ transversely along $C$, with $H^1(S,N_{S/X})=H^1(S,N_{S/X}(-C))=0$ (with $X$ a smooth projective model of $\overline{\M}_g$ near $S$). Then Claim P implies: for every holomorphic arc $(S_\epsilon)$ of surfaces in $X$ with $S_0=S$, transverse to $D$, whose trace curves $C_\epsilon:=S_\epsilon\cap D$ satisfy "$\pi_i(C_\epsilon)$ is a single point $Y_\epsilon$", one has $Y_\epsilon\equiv Y_0$. In particular, by Theorem 5.3, Claim P implies that for every $\xi\in T_{Y_0}\M_{g_i,n_i}\setminus\{0\}$ and every lift $v(\xi)$, NO arc with velocity $v(\xi)$ has collapsed traces. This is rigidity assertion (R).
